Higher order expansion for the probabilistic local well-posedness theory for a cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation Thanks: J.-B.C. supported by FCT — Fundação para a Ciência e a Tecnologia, under the project: UIDB/04561/2020 Thanks: J. F. was partially supported by the grant NSF-DMS-1816408
Jean-Baptiste Casteras, Juraj Földes, Gennady Uraltsev

Abstract
. In this paper, we study the probabilistic local well-posedness of the cubic Schrödinger equation (cubic NLS): ( i ∂ t + Δ ) u = ± | u | 2 u on [ 0 , T ) × ℝ d , (i\partial_{t}+\Delta)u=\pm|u|^{2}u\text{ on }[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}, with initial data being a Wiener randomization at unit scale of a given function f f . We prove that a solution exists almost-surely locally in time provided f ∈ H x S ​ ( ℝ d ) f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) with S > max ⁡ ( d − 3 4 , d − 4 2 ) S>\max\big(\frac{d-3}{4},\frac{d-4}{2}\big) for d ≥ 3 d\geq 3 . In particular, we establish that the local well-posedness holds for any S > 0 S>0 when d = 3 d=3 . We also show that, under appropriate smallness conditions for the initial data, the solutions are global in time and scatter. The solutions are constructed as a sum of an explicit multilinear expansion of the flow in terms of the random initial data and of an additional smoother remainder term with deterministically subcritical regularity. This construction allows us to introduce a new and refined notion of graded scattering. We develop the framework of directional space-time norms to control the (probabilistic) multilinear expansion and the (deterministic) remainder term and to obtain improved bilinear probabilistic-deterministic Strichartz estimates.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q41, 37L50

1.
Introduction

In this paper we study the almost-sure local well-posedness of the
cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation:

\begin{cases}(i\partial_{t}+\Delta)u=\pm|u|^{2}u\qquad\text{on}\quad[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
u(0)=f\in H_{x}^{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\end{cases}

in one temporal and
d\geq 3
spatial dimensions. We assume that the
initial datum
f
belongs to the Sobolev space
H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with

S\geq 0
(see (
•
‣
1.4
) below for the definition) and the
solution
u
is a function in

C^{0}\big([0,T);H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
for some
T>0
that solves
(
1
) in a mild sense (see the discussion
above (
1
)). The equation is invariant
under the transformation

u_{\lambda}(t,x)=\lambda u(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda x),\quad\lambda>0,

which also conserves the homogeneous Sobolev
norm
\dot{H}^{\mathfrak{s}_{c}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\mathfrak{s}_{c}:=\frac{d-2}{2}
, of the initial condition
u_{\lambda}(0,x)=\lambda f(\lambda x)
:

\|u_{\lambda}(0)\|_{\dot{H}^{\mathfrak{s}_{c}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\big|\Delta^{\mathfrak{s}_{c}/2}(\lambda f(\lambda x))\big|^{2}dx\Big)^{1/2}=\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{\mathfrak{s}_{c}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

This suggests that the regularity exponent
\mathfrak{s}_{c}
is critical for
the well-posedness of the equation. Indeed, it is known that when

S\geq\mathfrak{s}_{c}\coloneqq\frac{d-2}{2}
a solution to (
1
)
exists at least locally in time, that is,
(
1
) is locally well-posed, (see for instance

[
Caz03
,
CW90
,
CKSTT08
,
RV07
,
Pau07
,
PS10
]
).
On the other hand, Christ, Colliander, and Tao in
[
CCT03
]

established that (
1
) is locally ill-posed if

S<\mathfrak{s}_{c}
. More precisely, there are solutions with an arbitrary small
initial datum in
H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which are unbounded on arbitrarily small
time intervals.

In the present paper, we show that the ill-posedness is
probabilistically exceptional when
S>S_{\mathrm{min}}
with

S_{\mathrm{min}}<\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, given by

\begin{aligned} S_{\mathrm{min}}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }d=3,\\
\frac{1}{4}&\text{if }d=4,\\
\frac{d-4}{2}&\text{if }d\geq 5,\end{cases}\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} \mathfrak{s}_{c}\coloneqq\frac{d-2}{2}.\end{aligned}

In other words, if we randomly choose the initial condition

f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then with probability one, problem
(
1
) is locally well-posed.

The deterministic nonlinear Schrödinger equation
(
1
) has been the subject of extensive study
since the late 1970s due to its physical relevance as a model for
dispersive wave-like systems, especially in their asymptotic regimes,
for example many interacting Bosonic particle systems (Bose-Einstein
condensates), non-linear optics, and small water waves (see
e.g.
[
EY01
,
CSS92
]
). The
equation (
1
) is also a model problem for a large
class of “dispersive” PDEs, and it is a standard example of an
infinite dimensional Hamiltonian system

[
MNPRS20
]
.

Bourgain’s seminal work

[
Bou94
]
attracted interest to
probabilistic aspects of (
1
), and revealed
new connections to other fields. Specifically, there is the desire to
study local and global dynamics of (
1
) with
initial data given by a probability distribution derived from
constructive quantum field theory, such as the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model. This
naturally leads to study (
1
) with
low-regularity, random initial conditions. Significant results were
obtained for (
1
) on a torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, by Deng,
Nahmod, Yue, who proved in

[
DNY21
,
DNY22
]

well-posedness and, for
\mathbb{T}^{3}
, the invariance of the associated
Gibbs measures. Analogous results for the cubic wave equation on

\mathbb{T}^{3}
were obtained by the same authors together with Bringmann in

[
BDNY22
]
, providing a wave equation
analogue to Hairer’s (later with Matetski) celebrated result on
regularity structures

[
Hai14
,
HM18
]

for parabolic equations. On
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the understanding of randomized
local and global dynamics of (
1
) is more
limited, with important partial progress obtained in

[
BOP15
,
PW18
,
BT08a
,
Spi21
,
SSW21
,
SSW21a
,
Cam23
,
DLM19
]
.
In one-dimension (
d=1
) Burq, Thomann, and Tzvetkov in

[
BTT13
]
proved the existence and invariance of
the Gibbs measure via probabilistic methods for a Nonlinear
Schrödinger equation with an additional coercive potential.

Our present work improves the local well-posedness theory of all the
above works on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\geq 3
. For a more in-depth overview of the
history and motivation, we direct the reader to
Section

1.2
.

In this manuscript and widely accepted in literature, a solution of
(
1
) is a function

u\in C^{0}\big([0,T);H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
that is a fixed point of the
Duhamel iteration map
u\mapsto\mathbb{I}_{f}(|u|^{2}u)
, where

\mathbb{I}_{f}(h)\coloneqq e^{it\Delta}f(x)\mp i\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)\Delta}h(s,x)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}s\,,

with the sign in front of the integral being opposite to the sign of
the right-hand side of (
1
). Next, let us
state our first main result.

Theorem 1.1

(Local
well-posedness)
.

Fix
f\in H_{x}^{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
S_{\mathrm{min}}<S<\mathfrak{s}_{c}
(defined in
(
1
)). Then, with probability
1
the equation

\begin{cases}(i\partial_{t}+\Delta)u=\pm|u|^{2}u&\text{on }[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
u(0)=\mathfrak{f}.\end{cases}

admits a solution
u\in C^{0}\big([0,T),H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
, for a random
time
T>0
satisfying for appropriate
C,c>0
the bound

\mathbb{P}(T>t)\geq 1-C\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{-c}}{C\|f\|_{H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}}\Big)

for any
t\in(0,1)
.

The random initial datum
\mathfrak{f}\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the unit-scale Wiener
randomization of the function
f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
given by
(
1.1
) below (see

Section

1.1
for details).

Theorem

1.1
improves on known results for
probabilistic well-posedness on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for all dimensions

d\geq 3
. In the case of physical relevance
d=3
, we obtain
the optimal result except, possibly, for the endpoint

S_{\mathrm{min}}=0
. Indeed, if
f
belongs to
H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with

S<0
, then a solution
u
to (
1
) would
reside in
C^{0}\big([0;T);H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
. Consequently,

u(t)
for any
t\in[0,T)
would, a priori, be merely a distribution,
and the nonlinearity
|u(t)|^{2}u(t)
would not be defined. A
probabilistic scaling argument (see
[
DNY22
, Section
1.2]
) suggests that the natural
regularity endpoint for probabilistic local well-posedness results is

\mathfrak{s}_{\mathrm{pr}}=-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
in all dimensions. However, the above
discussion shows that for
S<0
the equation
(
1
) needs to be appropriately renormalized,
and, unlike on compact spatial domains, this process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is not
well understood.

If
S>S_{\mathrm{min}}
as in
Theorem

1.1
, we
also obtain global well-posedness and scattering if
\mathfrak{f}
is
sufficiently small, as detailed in
Theorem

1.2

below. We say a solution
u
scatters in the space
H_{x}^{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

if, asymptotically,
u
behaves like the solution to the linear
Schrödinger equation, that is, there exists a function

\mathfrak{w}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\|u(t)-e^{it\Delta}\mathfrak{w}\|_{H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0\,.

Global well-posedness and scattering for large initial data requires different techniques and assumptions that we do not address in this manuscript.

Theorem 1.2

(Global well-posedness and scattering for small initial data)
.

Under the assumptions of

Theorem

1.1
, there exists a set

\Omega_{\mathrm{glob}}
with

\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathrm{glob}})>1-C\exp\Bigg(-\frac{1}{c\|f\|_{H^{S+2\varepsilon}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}}\Bigg)

with
C,c>0
depending only on
S
and
\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, such that

T=+\infty
on
\Omega_{\mathrm{glob}}
, where
T
is the existence time of the
solution from
Theorem

1.1
. In addition, such
a solution
u\in C^{0}\big([0,+\infty),H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
, scatters in

H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, that is, almost surely on
\Omega_{\mathrm{glob}}
there
exists
\mathfrak{w}\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
(
1
) holds. Furthermore, scattering
(
1
) holds in higher regularity
norms upon subtracting from the solution
u
an explicit time dependent
function, as detailed in
Corollary

1.5
.

Our proof of Theorem
1.1
and

1.2
relies on a multilinear expansion of the
solution
u\in C^{0}\big([0,T);H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
to
(
1.1
) of the form

u(t)=\sum_{k\leq M}\mathfrak{z}_{k}(t)+u^{\#}_{M}(t)\quad\text{with the remainder}\quad u^{\#}_{M}(t)\coloneqq u(t)-\sum_{k\leq M}\mathfrak{z}_{k}(t),

for a fixed, sufficiently large,
M\in\mathbb{N}
. Each
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
can be
explicitly expressed as a
k
-linear expression of
\mathfrak{f}
by using
point-wise products and compositions with the linear
evolution. Although these formulas are explicit, they quickly become
complicated for
k>1
. Specifically,
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
are defined inductively
by setting
\mathfrak{z}_{0}=0
,
\mathfrak{z}_{1}(t)\coloneqq e^{it\Delta}\mathfrak{f}
, and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{z}_{k+1}(t)\coloneqq\mp i\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=k+1\\
0\leq k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\leq k\end{subarray}}}\hskip 25.00003pt\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)\Delta}\mathfrak{z}_{k_{1}}\overline{\mathfrak{z}_{k_{2}}}\mathfrak{z}_{k_{3}}(s)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}s.

Substituting the expansion (
1
) into
(
1.1
) gives us an equation for the remainder

u^{\#}_{M}(t)
and we
require
that for an appropriate
M
, we
have
u^{\#}_{M}\in C^{0}\big([0,T),H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
for some

\mathfrak{s}>\mathfrak{s}_{c}
. If
M=1
, the solution has the form

u=\mathfrak{z}_{1}+u^{\#}_{1}
; in the literature this is called Bourgain’s
trick
[
Bou94
]
or Da
Prato-Debussche trick

[
DPD02
]
. The decomposition

u=\mathfrak{z}_{1}+u^{\#}_{1}
is natural, because the linear Schrödinger
evolution does not have a smoothing effect: there is no gain in
regularity for
\mathfrak{z}_{1}
compared to
\mathfrak{f}
. In fact, we show that the
remainder
u^{\#}_{1}
term and the higher order terms
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
,

k>1
, have better regularity than
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{z}_{1}
, especially
when
S\ll\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, that is, when
\mathfrak{f}
is rough.

Requiring that
u
has the form (
1
) with

u^{\#}_{M}\in C^{0}\big([0,T),H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
restricts the notion
of the solution. Indeed,
Theorem

1.1
does
not provide uniqueness of solutions in the class

C^{0}\big([0;T);H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
, while uniqueness does hold for
solutions of the form (
1
).

The next theorem summarizes the regularity and scattering results for
the multilinear correction terms
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Theorem 1.3

(Regularity and scattering for
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
)
.

Fix
f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
S>0
, let

\mathfrak{f}\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the unit-scale Wiener randomization of the
function
f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
given by
(
1.1
), and for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
define

\mu(k,S)\coloneqq\min\Big(kS,2S+\frac{1}{2},S+1\Big).

The following properties hold almost-surely for the multilinear
expansion terms
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
, defined inductively by the relation
(
1
):

Regularity:

For any
\widetilde{S}<\mu(k,S)
it holds that
\mathfrak{z}_{k}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R};H^{\widetilde{S}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and there exists
C=C(\widetilde{S},k,S)
such that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\|\mathfrak{z}_{k}\|_{C^{0}_{t}(\mathbb{R};H^{\widetilde{S}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}>\lambda\Big)\leq C\exp\Bigg(-\frac{\lambda^{\frac{2}{k}}}{C\|f\|_{H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}}\Bigg)\,.

Scattering:

For any
\widetilde{S}<\mu(k,S)
the term
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
scatters in
H^{\widetilde{S}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, that is, there exists random
\mathfrak{w}_{k}\in\bigcap_{\widetilde{S}<\mu(k,S)}H^{\widetilde{S}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and a deterministic constant
C=C(k,\widetilde{S},S)
such that

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\big\|\mathfrak{z}_{k}(t)-e^{it\Delta}\mathfrak{w}_{k}\big\|_{H^{\widetilde{S}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0,

and

\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\|\mathfrak{w}_{k}\big\|_{H^{\widetilde{S}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}>\lambda\Big)\leq C\exp\Bigg(-\frac{\lambda^{\frac{2}{k}}}{C\|f\|_{H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}}\Bigg).

We believe that if
\mu(k,S)=kS
, then

Theorem

1.3
is optimal except for
the endpoint case. For example
\mu(3,S)\leq 3S
, as has already observed in

[
SSW21a
]
: if

\widehat{f}(\xi)=|\ell|^{-S}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{1/2}(\ell)}(\xi)
for

\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
large, then

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\|\mathfrak{z}_{3}(t)\|_{H^{3S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<+\infty
only if

f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Concerning the endpoint, it is reasonable to
expect that
\mathfrak{z}_{k}\in C^{0}\big(\mathbb{R};H^{\mu(k,S)}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
but such
result would not improve our main results. On the other hand, proving
that
\mathfrak{z}_{k}\in C^{0}\big(\mathbb{R};H^{\widetilde{S}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
for some

\widetilde{S}>\mu(k,S)
when
kS>\min\big(2S+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},S+1\big)
would lead to
improvements on
S_{\mathrm{min}}
for
d\geq 4
in our main theorems.

The minimal initial regularity
S_{\mathrm{min}}
is chosen such that

\min\big(2S_{\mathrm{min}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},S_{\mathrm{min}}+1\big)=\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, and
therefore since

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\mu(k,S)=\min\big(2S+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},S+1\big)
,

Theorem

1.3
implies that if

f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
S>S_{\mathrm{min}}
then

\mathfrak{z}_{k}\in C^{0}\big(\mathbb{R};H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
with

\mathfrak{s}>\mathfrak{s}_{c}
for all sufficiently large
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The following result
refines
Theorem

1.1
by asserting that the
local in time solution
u
for (
1.1
) of the form
(
1
) with

u^{\#}_{M}\in C^{0}\big([0,T),H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
for some

\mathfrak{s}>\mathfrak{s}_{c}
is unique, exists for a maximal time interval

T_{\mathrm{max}}
, and scatters in
H^{\mathfrak{s}_{c}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if the initial
data is small enough.

Theorem 1.4

(Regularity and scattering of
u^{\#}_{M}
)
.

Fix
S
with
S_{\mathrm{min}}<S<\mathfrak{s}_{c}
and let
M\in\mathbb{N}
be such that

\mu(M+1,S)>\mathfrak{s}_{c}
.
Then for any
\mathfrak{s}<\mu(M+1,S)
the following properties hold almost-surely.

•

There exists a random existence time
T\in(0,+\infty]
such that (
1.1
) admits a solution
u\in C^{0}\big([0,T);H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
of the form (
1
) with

u^{\#}_{M}\in C^{0}\big([0,T);H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
.

•

It holds that
u^{\#}_{M}\in C^{0}([0,T);H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

•

Any two solutions with
u^{\#}_{M}\in C^{0}([0,T);H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
coincide on
[0,T)
.

•

The time of existence satisfies
T=+\infty
on
\Omega_{\mathrm{glob}}
as in
Theorem

1.2
. Moreover,

u^{\#}_{M}
scatters in
H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, that is, there exists

\mathfrak{w}_{M}^{\#}\in\bigcap_{\mathfrak{s}<\mu(M+1,S)}H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\big\|u^{\#}_{M}-e^{it\Delta}\mathfrak{w}_{M}^{\#}\big\|_{H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0\,,

and

\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\|\mathfrak{w}_{M}^{\#}\big\|_{H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}>\lambda\Big)\leq C\exp\Bigg(-\frac{\lambda^{\frac{2}{k}}}{C\|f\|_{H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}}\Bigg).

If we combine
Theorem

1.3
and

Theorem

1.4
, then we obtain, information
about the behavior of
u
as
t\to\infty
that generalizes scattering from
(
1
). In particular, in the next corollary
we summarize the scattering results obtained above and we deduce scattering
in more regular spaces
H^{\mu(M+1,S)}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by removing explicit
higher order correction terms
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
,
k\leq M
from the solution. The
non-explicit remainder term
u^{\#}_{M}
scatters in deterministically
subcritical regularity
H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mathfrak{s}>\mathfrak{s}_{c}
.

Corollary 1.5

(Graded scattering)
.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem

1.4
, on

\Omega_{\mathrm{glob}}
there exists a unique global solution

u\in C^{0}([0,+\infty);H^{S}_{d}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
to (
1.1
) of
the form

u=\sum_{k\leq M}\mathfrak{z}_{k}(t)+u^{\#}_{M}

and the terms
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
and
u^{\#}_{M}
scatter, as described by
(
Scattering:
) and (
•
‣
1.4
),
respectively. It follows that for any for any
M^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
there exists

\mathfrak{w}_{>M^{\prime}}^{\#}\in\bigcap_{\widetilde{S}<\mu(M^{\prime}+1,S)}H^{\widetilde{S}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\Big\|\big(u-\sum_{k=1}^{M^{\prime}}\mathfrak{z}_{k}(t)\big)-e^{it\Delta}\mathfrak{w}_{>M^{\prime}}^{\#}\Big\|_{H^{\widetilde{S}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0\qquad\text{for all}\quad\widetilde{S}<\mu(M^{\prime}+1,S)\,.

Theorem

1.4
summarizes our results in
classical spaces
C^{0}\big([0,T),H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
. The crucial
novelty of our paper is the introduction of new family of directional
space-time norms, defined in (
2
), that
prove to be more efficient at capturing dispersion and regularity
properties of
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
. Indeed, once the directional space-time
spaces
X^{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T))
and
Y^{S}([0,T))
are defined (see
(
3
), (
3
)), Theorems

1.1
–

1.4
can be reformulated more
precisely and deterministically.

Specifically, given a deterministic initial condition

f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we define the multilinear data

\vec{z}_{M}=(z_{k})_{k\in\{1,\ldots,M\}}
of order
M
associated to

f
inductively, by setting
z_{0}=0
,
z_{1}(t)\coloneqq e^{it\Delta}f
and

\displaystyle z_{k+1}(t)\coloneqq\mp i\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=k+1\\
0\leq k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\leq k\end{subarray}}}\hskip 22.0pt\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)\Delta}(z_{k_{1}}\overline{z_{k_{2}}}z_{k_{3}})(s)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}s\,,\quad k\geq 2\,.

Notice that if
f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
S<\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, then

z_{k}
for
k\geq 2
may not be well defined in general. However, we
assume that
z_{k}
are well defined, as for example, in the case when

f\in H^{S}_{x}
with
S>\mathfrak{s}_{c}
. Then the multilinear data

\vec{z}_{M}
can be viewed as an enrichment of the initial condition

f
, in the spirit of Lyon’s theory of rough paths

[
Lyo98
,
LCL07
]
or Hairer’s regularity
structures, see
[
FH20
,
Hai14
]
, and references therein. The
central assertion of the following

Theorem

1.6
then becomes
the continuity of the map
(f,\vec{z}_{M})\mapsto u
in appropriate
low-regularity spaces. We emphasize that
\vec{z}_{M}
depends on
f
. In
addition, we provide of lower bound on the existence time of
u
in
terms of suitable norms of
f
and
\vec{z}_{M}
.

Below
Theorem

1.6
, we
introduce Wiener randomization of the initial data and we show that
almost surely the functions
z_{k}
are well defined and bounded in the
required spaces.

The next two theorems contain precise formulation of the described
heuristics, and they are the main results of our paper from which
Theorems
1.1
,

1.2
,

1.3
, and

1.4
are deduced. For the formulation
of the statements we used the notation
\lesssim
defined in

Section

1.4
and the norms of the
Y^{S}
spaces defined in
(
3
).

Theorem 1.6

(Deterministic well-posedness for rough data)
.

Fix
S>S_{\mathrm{min}}
,
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mu(M+1,S)>\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, and then

\mathfrak{s}_{c}<\mathfrak{s}<\min\big(\mu(M+1,S),\mathfrak{s}_{c}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\big)
. Fix any

f\in H^{S+\varepsilon}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and any
T_{0}\in[0,+\infty]
for which the
associated multilinear data

\vec{z}_{M}=(z_{k})_{k\in{1,\ldots,M}}
, defined a-priori by
(
1
), satisfy

(1.15)

\|z_{k}\|_{Y^{\mu(k,S)+\varepsilon}([0,T_{0}))}<\infty\qquad\text{ for all }k\in\{1,\ldots,M\}\,.

For any
T>0
and any function
u
with the domain

[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
we define
u^{\#}_{M}
as

u=z_{\leq M}+u^{\#}_{M},\quad z_{\leq M}\coloneqq\sum_{k\leq M}z_{k}.

Then for any
0<\varepsilon\lesssim_{M,\mathfrak{s},S}1
and any

0<\varepsilon_{0}\lesssim_{\varepsilon,M,\mathfrak{s},S}1
, appearing in the definition of the norms

X^{\mathfrak{s}}
and
Y^{S}
(see (
3
) and
(
3
)), the following assertions hold.

Local existence of solutions:

There exists
T\in(0,T_{0}]
and a solution
u
to (
1
) with

\|u^{\#}_{M}\|_{X^{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T))}+\|u^{\#}_{M}\|_{C^{0}\big([0,T);H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)}<\infty.

The time of existence
T
admits the lower bound

T\geq\min\Big(T_{0},\frac{1}{C}\big\|1+\big(\max_{k\leq M}\|z_{k}\|_{Y^{\mu(k,S)+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R})}\big)^{3}\big\|^{-\nicefrac{{3}}{{c}}}\Big)\,,

for some constants
C=C(\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},M,\mathfrak{s},S)>1
and
c=c(\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{0},M,\mathfrak{s},S)>0
.

Time-continuity of solutions:

Any solutions
u
to (
1
), defined on the time interval
[0,T)
, with
\|u^{\#}_{M}\|_{X^{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T))}<\infty
satisfies

\|u^{\#}_{M}\|_{C^{0}\big([0,T);H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)}\lesssim\|u^{\#}_{M}\|_{X^{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T))}.

Uniqueness of solutions:

Any two solutions
u_{1},u_{2}
to (
1
), defined on the time interval
[0,T)
, with
\|u^{\#}_{j,M}\|_{C^{0}\big([0,T);H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)}<\infty
,
j=1,2
coincide.

Blow-up criterion:

Let
T_{\mathrm{max}}
be the least upper bound of
T\leq T_{0}
for which a local
solution as above exists on
[0,T)
. Then either
T_{\mathrm{max}}=T_{0}
or

\displaystyle\|u^{\#}_{M}\|_{X^{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T_{\max}))}=\lim_{t\to T_{\mathrm{max}}}\|u^{\#}_{M}(t)\|_{H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}=+\infty.

Global existence of solutions:

There exists
0<\delta_{0}\lesssim_{\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon,M,\mathfrak{s},S}\!1
such that if

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{S+\varepsilon}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\delta_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\|z_{k}\|_{Y^{\mu(k,S)+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R})}<\delta_{0}\qquad\text{for all }k\in\{1,\ldots,M\}\,,

then
T_{\mathrm{max}}=+\infty
.

Time-continuity and scattering of multilinear data:

It holds that

(1.21)

\|z_{1}\|_{C^{0}\big([0,T_{0});H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

and for any
k\geq 2
,

\|z_{k}\|_{C^{0}\big([0,T_{0});H^{\mu(k,S)}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)}\lesssim\max_{k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}}\|z_{k}\|_{Y^{\mu(k^{\prime},S)+\varepsilon}([0,T_{0}))}.

If
T_{0}=+\infty
then
z_{k}
,
k\leq M
, scatter in

H^{\mu(k,S)}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, that is, there exist

w_{k}\in H^{\mu(k,S)}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\big\|z_{k}-e^{it\Delta}w_{k}\big\|_{H^{\mu(k,S)}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0.

Scattering of global solutions:

Under the assumptions
(
Global existence of solutions:
) for global existence,

u^{\#}_{M}
scatters in
H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, that is, there exists

w^{\#}_{M}\in H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\big\|u^{\#}_{M}-e^{it\Delta}w_{M}^{\#}\big\|_{H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0.

Furthermore, the mapping from the enriched initial condition to the
solution is locally Lipschitz-continuous in the following sense. For
fixed parameters as above, define the distance on the initial data as

d(f,g)\coloneqq\|f-g\|_{H^{S+\varepsilon}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\max_{k\in{1,\ldots,M}}\|\vec{z}_{M}(f)_{k}-\vec{z}_{M}(g)_{k}\|_{Y^{\mu(k,S)}([0,T_{0}))}\,,

with
\vec{z}_{M}(f)
and
\vec{z}_{M}(g)
being the multilinear data
associated to
f
and
g
according to
(
1
). Then for any
R>0
and corresponding time of existence

T=\begin{cases}\min\Big(T_{0},\frac{1}{C}\big(1+R^{3}\big)^{-\nicefrac{{3}}{{c}}}\Big)&\text{if }R\geq\delta_{0}\,,\\
T=T_{0}&\text{if }R<\delta_{0}\,,\end{cases}

the map from
(f,\vec{z}_{M})
with
d(0,f)\leq R
to the remainder term

u^{\#}_{M}\in X^{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T))\cap C^{0}([0,T);H^{\mathfrak{s}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

is Lipschitz-continuous.

In
Theorem

1.6
the
initial datum
f\in H^{S+\varepsilon}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is deterministic, but there are
additional conditions on
\vec{z}_{M}
. If
f
is randomized, then the
following theorem provides a refinement of

Theorem

1.3
, showing that
almost-surely estimates on

\|\mathfrak{z}_{k}\|_{Y^{\mu(k,S)+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R})}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, hold, as required by

Theorem

1.6
.

Theorem 1.7

(Probabilistic estimates on multilinear expansions)
.

Fix any
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
S>0
, any
0<\varepsilon\lesssim_{M,S}1
, and any

0<\varepsilon_{0}\lesssim_{\varepsilon,M,S}1
, appearing in the definition of the norm

Y^{S}
(see (
3
)). The following assertions
hold.

For any
f\in H^{S+\varepsilon}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
let
\mathfrak{f}
be its unit-scale Wiener
randomization (see
Section

1.1
) and let

\mathfrak{z}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, be the multilinear expansions defined by
(
1
).

Then there exists
C=C(\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon,M,S)>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\|\mathfrak{z}_{k}\|_{Y^{\mu(k,S)}(\mathbb{R})}>\lambda\Big)\leq C\exp\Bigg(-\frac{\lambda^{\frac{2}{k}}}{C\|f\|_{H^{S+\varepsilon}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}}\Bigg)\,,

for any
k\leq M
with
\mu(k,S)
defined in (
1.3
).

1.1.
The unit-scale Wiener randomization

Next, we describe the unit-scale Wiener randomization
\mathfrak{f}
of the
initial condition
f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. First, we fix a sequence

(g_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
of i.i.d. complex valued random variables on a
probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})
and we assume that all their moments
are bounded, that is,
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\big[|g_{k}|^{p}\big]<\infty
for all

p\in\mathbb{N}
. For example, these assumptions are satisfied when

g_{k}
are independent, standard (unit variance and
0
mean) complex
Gaussian random variables; the reader may assume this to be the case.

Let
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
be an even, non-negative cut-off function
supported in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
centered at
0
and such that, for
all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\psi(\xi-k)=1.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we define the operators
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{Q}}\mathopen{}_{k}
by setting

(Q_{k}f)(x)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big(\psi(\xi-k)\widehat{f}(\xi)\big)(x),\quad\text{for }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,,

where
\mathcal{F}(f)
stands for the Fourier transform of

f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\;S\geq 0
. We then set

\mathfrak{f}\coloneqq f^{\omega}\coloneqq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}g_{k}(\omega)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{Q}}\mathopen{}_{k}f.

The random function
\mathfrak{f}
(random variable valued in functions on

\mathbb{R}^{d}
) is then used as an initial condition in
(
1.1
).

The randomization
f\mapsto\mathfrak{f}
does not change (and in particular does
not improve) the differentiability properties of
f
, in the sense
that

\|f\|_{H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\approx\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\|\mathfrak{f}\|_{H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2},\qquad\text{for all }S\in\mathbb{R},

as it follows from a direct application of Plancherel’s formula and
the independence. Thus if

f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\setminus H^{S+\varepsilon}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some

\varepsilon>0
, then

\mathfrak{f}\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\setminus H^{S+\varepsilon}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
almost-surely (see

[
BT08
]
). However, the integrability
properties of
\mathfrak{f}
are improved compared to
f
(see

Proposition

6.1
).

We interpret the infinite sum in (
1.1
)
as the limit in
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}_{\Omega}H^{S}_{x}}
for any

p<\infty
of finite partial sums. Equivalently, we define
(
1.1
) for functions

f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with compact Fourier support and then extend the
definition by continuity. It would be interesting to understand
whether alternative approximation procedures may yield different
distributions of multilinear terms
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
, defined by
(
1
).

1.2.
Motivation and history

Next, we present an overview of the history and motivation for the
problem (
1
) and its probabilistic
counterpart (
1.1
), leading to our
result. The deterministic nonlinear Schrödinger equation
(
1
) possesses several remarkable
mathematical features. First, the equation
(
1
) is “dispersive”, meaning that
solution’s components supported on disjoint parts of the frequency
spectrum propagate with different velocities. Second, both the linear Schrödinger equation
(i\partial_{t}+\Delta)u=0
and the nonlinear
Schrödinger equation
(
1
) are infinite
dimensional Hamiltonian systems. The linear equation has a Hamiltonian

\mathrm{H}_{0}[u]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u(x)|^{2}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}x
while the nonlinear one is
associated with the Hamiltonian

\mathrm{H}[u]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u(x)|^{2}\pm\frac{1}{4}|u(x)|^{4}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}x
. For a
comprehensive discussion of the Hamiltonian structure of Schrödinger equations, we refer the reader to

[
MNPRS20
]
.

Equation (
1
) is known to be locally
well-posed for
S\geq\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, where we recall that
\mathfrak{s}_{c}\coloneqq\frac{d-2}{2}
is known as the
critical scaling regularity (see for instance

[
Caz03
,
CW90
,
CKSTT08
,
RV07
,
Pau07
,
PS10
]
). Conversely,
ill-posedness for
S<\mathfrak{s}_{c}
has been established by Christ, Colliander,
and Tao
[
CCT03
]
. The local well-posedness
of (
1
) is obtained by finding a fixed point of
the Duhamel iteration map (
1
), which in turn follows from the Banach fixed point theorem in appropriate norms that capture the dispersive
nature of (
1
). An example of such norms are
the Strichartz space-time norms (see
Section

2
),
and the associated bounds are called Strichartz estimates (see

Lemma

2.1
below and originally in
[
Str77
]
).

An interest in the probabilistic aspects of physical equations, which were
originally formulated deterministically, can be traced back at least
to the work of Poincaré
[
Poi07
, Chapter 1 Section
IV]
who observed that regardless of the precise knowledge of the laws of physics, the
initial conditions can only be known approximately. This inherent
uncertainty profoundly influences the observed behavior of a physical
system, especially for systems that are naturally described as
statistical limits. The nonlinear Schrödinger equation, which is the
focus of this paper, is one such equation that can be derived (see

[
FKSS17
,
MNPRS20
]
, and references
therein) as the asymptotic limit of certain physical systems, such as
a large number of interacting Bosonic particles, or as the equation
for water waves in the small amplitude regime. Therefore, it is
natural to investigate properties of solutions with initial conditions
governed by probability distributions. In fact, our result, stated in

Theorem

1.1
, suggests that the carefully
constructed blow-up solutions of
[
CCT03
]
with initial data in

H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
S<\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, are statistically irrelevant, and the
physical systems described by the nonlinear Schrödinger equation are
well-behaved even in low regularity regimes.

NLS on the torus

The effect of randomization of the initial condition on improving
local (or global) well-posedness was pioneered by Bourgain in the
context of the cubic NLS on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. He established
almost-sure local well-posedness of (
1
) on

\mathbb{T}^{1}
(
[
Bou94
]
) and on

\mathbb{T}^{2}
(
[
Bou96
]
). The
introduction of randomized initial conditions was related to
constructing a Gibbs measure: a probability measure on

H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for appropriate
S
, which is invariant under the
flow of the equation. Bourgain used almost-sure local well-posedness
and the invariance of the Gibbs measure to obtain almost-sure global
well-posedness of the cubic NLS. The Gibbs measure is supported on
spaces
H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with regularity

S<S_{\mathrm{Gibbs}}\coloneqq 1-\frac{d}{2}
, and therefore it is essential to
understand the equation with very rough, random initial conditions.
Recently, Deng, Nahmod and Yue in

[
DNY19
]
extended the result

[
Bou96
]
for
\mathbb{T}^{2}
to
arbitrary odd power nonlinearities. In

[
DNY22
]
, the same authors introduced
the theory of random tensors, which allowed for the proof of the
almost-sure local well-posedness for
d\geq 3
up to a natural
probabilistic regularity threshold
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathrm{pr}}=-\frac{1}{2}
for the
cubic NLS. The problem of almost-sure global existence in low
regularity essentially remains open in
d\geq 3
with particular interest
in the dimension
d=3
, where the probabilistic scaling

\mathfrak{s}_{\mathrm{pr}}=-\frac{1}{2}
coincides with the regularity threshold

S_{\mathrm{Gibbs}}\coloneqq 1-\frac{d}{2}
of the Gibbs measure. Later the problem
was solved in
[
BDNY22
]
for the wave
equation with
d=3
.

Bourgain’s techniques from

[
Bou94
,
Bou96
]
were later used to study
other equations on compact domains, such as the cubic wave equation

[
BT10
]
, or the Hartree NLS equation

[
Bou97
]
(see also

[
LRS88
,
Sy21
,
DNY21
,
BT08a
]

for other results in this direction). For a more detailed survey of
the known results for the nonlinear Schrödinger equation on compact
domains, we direct the interested reader to

[
Nah15
]
. We refer to

[
Ken20
]
for an overview of Bourgain’s seminal
contributions to the study of dispersive PDEs.

NLS on Euclidean space

If the domain is
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, as in the present manuscript, then the
dynamics of Schrödinger equation differs from the dynamics on compact
domains. In particular, on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, different frequency components of a
solution interact weakly after long enough time as they disperse in
space, which lead to local smoothing estimates (see

Lemma

2.5
below; also

[
CS88
]
and
[
LP15
, Theorem
4.3]
). On a torus the
solutions are spatially confined and local smoothing is not available.

Furthermore, on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
there is no countable basis of eigenfunctions
for the Laplacian, and therefore a canonical randomization of initial
conditions in
H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is less well-understood. Instead, it
is common to take the unit-scale Wiener randomization of a fixed
function
f\in H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see
(
1.1
) and the related discussion for
precise definitions), since it closely mimics the randomization on a
torus. We remark that other randomizations have also been considered:
for instance see

[
BT08a
,
Spi21
,
SSW21
]
.

The question of almost-sure local well-posedness studied in the
current manuscript is of primary importance and has a rich
history. The first local well-posedness result on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
Wiener-randomized initial data (as in
(
1.1
)) was obtained by Bényi, Oh, and
Pocovnicu
[
BOP15
]
, where the authors
established a result in the spirit of

Theorem

1.1
for
d\geq 3
with

S>\frac{d-1}{d+1}\frac{d-2}{2}
. In
[
SSW21a
]
Shen, Soffer, and
Wu improved the result for
d=3
covering the range

S\geq\frac{1}{6}
. The works
[
BOP15
,
SSW21a
]
rely on a fixed point argument for the map
(
1
) with solutions controlled in Bourgain spaces

X^{s,b}
or their variation-norm variants
V^{p}
and
U^{p}
,
introduced by Koch, Tataru, and collaborators
[
HHK09
,
HTT11
,
KTV14
]
. Dodson, Lührmann, and Mendelson
[
DLM19
]

used direction spaces introduced by Ionescu and Kenig

[
IK06
,
IK07
]
to extended the local well-posedness result
in
[
BOP15
]
for
d=4
to
S>\frac{1}{3}
.

The papers
[
Cam23
,
SSW21a
,
DLM19
]
, cited
above, also obtained global well-posedness results in a smaller range
of regularities or under additional geometric assumptions on the
initial data (for example for radial initial conditions). We do not address
the global existence of large solutions, leaving such extensions of
the current framework for future work. We also mention that Pocovnicu
and Wang
[
PW18
]
obtained almost-sure
local well-posedness of (
1.1
) for an
extended range of regularities but in
L^{p}
-based spaces with

p\neq 2
. However, their approach does not allow for a refinement in the
spirit of
Theorem

1.3
and

Theorem

1.4
as their remainder term has the
same regularity as the initial data.

The functional framework of
[
DLM19
]
served as
inspiration for
[
CFU22
]
, where the present
authors extended the result of

[
BOP15
]
in arbitrary dimensions to
the case of the Laplacian in (
1.1
) replaced by a
more general operator
\mathcal{L}
. For example in

[
CFU22
]
one considers operators of the form

\mathcal{L}=(-\Delta)^{\sigma/2}+\mathcal{L}^{\#}
with
\mathcal{L}^{\#}
being of lower order
operator. In
[
CFU22
]
we obtained an
almost-sure local existence for solutions to (
1.1
)
with the form

u(t)=\mathfrak{z}_{1}+u^{\#}_{1}

with
u^{\#}_{1}\in C\big([0,T);H_{x}^{\frac{d-\sigma}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
, provided that

S>\widetilde{S}_{\min}(\sigma,d)
for explicitly given
\widetilde{S}_{\min}(\sigma,d)
.
Specifically, for the Laplacian
\mathcal{L}=\Delta

\widetilde{S}_{\min}(2,d)=(d-2)\times\begin{cases}\frac{(d-3)}{2(d-1)}&d\geq 4\,,\\
\frac{1}{6}&\ d=3\,,\end{cases}

which was the most general result for the second order NLS, except for
the endpoint case in
d=3
proved in
[
SSW21a
]
. Our
theory of multilinear expansions given by

Theorem

1.3
suggests (and proves
for
\mathcal{L}=\Delta
) that, as long as one looks for solutions of the form

u=e^{it\mathcal{L}}\mathfrak{f}+u^{\#}_{1}
with

u^{\#}_{1}\in C\big([0,T);H_{x}^{\frac{d-\sigma}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
, the results in

[
CFU22
]
for
d\in\{3,4\}
are optimal, with exception of the endpoint regularities. Indeed,
u^{\#}_{1}
cannot be smoother than
\mathfrak{z}_{3}
which, as
already observed in
[
SSW21a
]
, does not belong to

C\big([0,T);H_{x}^{\frac{d-\sigma}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
for

f\in H^{\frac{d-\sigma}{6}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
given by

\widehat{f}(\xi)=|k|^{-\frac{d-\sigma}{6}}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{1}(k)}(\xi)
with large
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

In this paper, we lower the required regularity on the initial data by
including higher order expansion to the solution. The idea of going
beyond the first order expansion given by the Da Prato - Debussche -
Bourgain trick has multiple precedents in literature. Christ in

[
Chr09
]
gave the meaning to rough
solutions to the 1D cubic Schrödinger equation by expressing the
solution as an infinite multilinear series. Developing a correct
functional framework to deal with expansions of arbitrary order is, for
example, the base for Lyon’s theory of rough paths

[
LCL07
]
, for paracontrolled
distributions (see

[
GIP15
]
), for regularity
structures (see
[
Hai14
]
). In our
context, multilinear expansions have been adopted by Bényi, Oh, and
Pocovnicu
[
BOP19
]
for equation
(
1.1
) in dimension
d=3
. In their work, the
multilinear correction terms
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
are controlled in the Bourgain

X^{s,b}
spaces (introduced in

[
Bou94
]
). The methods in

[
BOP19
]
provide regularity estimates on

\mathfrak{z}_{k}
that are substantially worse compared to

Theorem

1.3
, necessitating
expansions of arbitrarily high order to prove almost-sure local
well-posedness in
H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
S>\frac{1}{6}
. In our
approach, we use directional norms to prove almost-sure local
well-posedness in
H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
S>0
if we use arbitrarily
high order expansions. However our results for
d\geq 5
and
d=4
use
respectively one and two terms in the expansion. This suggests that
an improvement of regularity estimates for
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
with
k\geq 3
in

d\geq 4
would lead to improvements in

Theorem

1.1
. A different, and more
elaborated form, of high-order expansions was given in

[
DNY22
]
, however such approach seems
to be limited to the torus.

1.3.
Outline of the paper

The solution to equation (
1.1
), postulated
by
Theorem

1.6
, is a fixed point of the
iteration map
u\mapsto\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{f}}(|u|^{3}u)
, defined in
(
1
), which can be found by Picard iterations
starting from an initial guess, for example

u_{0}(t,x)=0
. It is immediate to check that

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{f}}(0)=z_{1}=e^{it\Delta}f,\qquad\mathbb{I}_{f}\big(|z_{1}|^{2}z_{1}\big)=z_{1}+z_{3}.

For the next step, one has

\mathbb{I}_{f}\big(|z_{1}+z_{3}|^{2}(z_{1}+z_{3})\big)\neq z_{1}+z_{3}+z_{5}\,.

However, for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
the
n
-th iteration of the mapping

u\mapsto\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{f}}(|u|^{3}u)
starting with
u=0
is a linear combination
of
k
-linear,
k\leq 3^{n-1}
, operators
R_{\tau}
applied to the initial data

f
. Each operator
R_{\tau}
is characterized by a ternary tree
\tau
,
which are introduced in the first part of
Section

6
. We remark that the order of multilinearity
k
of
R_{\tau}
is the number of leaves of the ternary tree
\tau
.
Adding together all terms of a fixed multilinear order
k
, that is, all

R_{\tau}
with
\tau
having
k
leaves, we
obtain
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
described inductively in (
1
).

The main novelty of the present paper is the introduction of a family
of new directional space-time norms (
2
) to
control the multilinear correction terms
z_{k}
and the remainder
term
u^{\#}_{M}
. A special case of our directional spaces were already used in

[
CFU22
]
and there, they were inspired by
[
DLM19
]
that were, in turn, motivated by
[
IK06
,
IK07
]
. We stress that our spaces are more general and to authors’ knowledge were not yet used in the literature. Then, we define an appropriately weighted
combination of the directional space-time norms and classical
Strichartz norms on each Littlewood-Paley projection (see
(
2
)) to define two families of norms
X^{s}
and
Y^{s}
indexed by
a regularity parameter
s\in\mathbb{R}
(see (
3
) and
(
3
)). The spaces
Y^{s}
are well-suited to control
the explicit terms
R_{\tau}[f,\ldots,f]
and
z_{k}
, while the norms

X^{s}
are used to control the remainder term
u^{\#}_{M}
arising
from the decomposition (
1
). To relate our results to a more classical setting, we establish that the boundedness in
X^{s}
or

Y^{s}
implies boundedness in
C^{0}\big([0,T];H^{s}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
(see

Proposition

3.3
).

To prove
Theorem

1.6
we look for a solution
of the form (
1
) for
M
such that

\mu(M+1,k)>\mathfrak{s}_{c}
. The function
u
is a solution, that is, a fixed point
of (
1
) if and only if
u^{\#}_{M}
is a fixed point of
the map (see (
1
) for the definition of
\mathbb{I}_{f}
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{z,M}(u^{\#}_{M})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{0}\big(\Phi_{z_{\leq M}}[u^{\#}_{M}]+[z,z,z]_{>M}\big)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mp i\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)\Delta}\big(\Phi_{z_{\leq M}}[u^{\#}_{M}]+[z,z,z]_{>M}\big)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}s,

where

z_{\leq M}\coloneqq\sum_{k\leq M}z_{k}\,,\qquad[z,z,z]_{>M}\coloneqq\sum_{\mathclap{\hskip 16.38895pt\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}>M\\
k_{j}\leq M\end{subarray}}}z_{k_{1}}\overline{z_{k_{2}}}z_{k_{3}}\,,

and

\displaystyle\Phi_{z_{\leq M}}[u^{\#}_{M}]

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big|z_{\leq M}+u^{\#}_{M}\big|^{2}\big(z_{\leq M}+u^{\#}_{M}\big)-\big|z_{\leq M}\big|^{2}z_{\leq M}.

We show that
u^{\#}_{M}\mapsto\mathcal{J}_{z,M}(u^{\#}_{M})
is a contraction on
sufficiently small bounded sets of
X^{\mathfrak{s}}\big([0,T)\big)
. The smallness
can be guaranteed if one chooses
T>0
to be small enough.

We prove this in two steps. First, assuming that

u^{\#}_{M}\in X^{\mathfrak{s}}\big([0,T)\big)
, we estimate the non-linearity

h\coloneqq\big(\Phi_{z_{\leq M}}[u^{\#}_{M}]+[z,z,z]_{>M}\big)

in the space
X^{*,\mathfrak{s}+\varepsilon}\big([0,T)\big)
, which is formally
dual to
X^{\mathfrak{s}}\big([0,T)\big)
(see (
3
)). We show that
the map

u^{\#}_{M}\mapsto\big(\Phi_{z_{\leq M}}[u^{\#}_{M}]+[z,z,z]_{>M}\big)

from
X^{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T))
to
X^{*,\mathfrak{s}+\varepsilon}([0,T))
has small Lipshitz
constant if
T>0
is chosen small enough. A minor modification shows
that shows that this is the case also if
T=+\infty
and the initial
condition and
u^{\#}_{M}
have small norms. The required bound on

\Big\|\big(\Phi_{z_{\leq M}}[u^{\#}_{M}]+[z,z,z]_{>M}\big)\Big\|_{X^{*,\mathfrak{s}+\varepsilon}([0,T))}

is obtained through bilinear Strichartz estimates in

Lemma

4.1
.

Second we show that for any
h\in X^{*,\mathfrak{s}+\varepsilon}([0,T))
it holds that

\Big\|\mp i\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-s)\Delta}h(s,\cdot)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}s\Big\|_{X^{\mathfrak{s}}\big([0,T)\big)}\lesssim\|h\|_{X^{*,\mathfrak{s}+\varepsilon}\big([0,T)\big)}\,.

The estimate (
1.3
) follows from duality and a
Christ-Kiselev type argument

[
CK01
]
once we establish the
linear theory for the flow
f\mapsto e^{it\Delta}f
in the directional spaces

X^{s}\big([0,T)\big)
.

This implies the global well-posedness result contained in

Theorem

1.2
.

The role of bilinear Strichartz estimates in the study of the
nonlinear Schrödinger equation is widely recognized. Our directional
spaces crucially allow us to deduce

L^{2}_{t,x}\times L^{2}_{t,x}\mapsto L^{2}_{t,x}
Strichartz estimates with a
gain of up to
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
derivative as long as one of the
functions is randomized, or more generally, bounded in the
Y^{S^{\prime}}

norm. The proof of bilinear Strichartz estimates

Lemma

4.1
for functions localized on fixed
Littlewood Paley annuli is surprisingly simple. One of the major
technical steps of the proof consists of combining these localized
estimates by summing over all dyadic frequency bands. We refer to

Proposition

4.2
for these details. We believe the study
of such directional spaces has independent interest for multilinear
Fourier extension estimates in Harmonic analysis. We recently learned
of a directional norms, similar to ours, were independently studied by
Beltran and Vega in
[
BV20
]
.

As already mentioned above, the previous works

[
BOP15
,
BOP19
,
SSW21a
]

used variants of Bourgain spaces, however they seem to
provide suboptimal regularity estimates in higher dimensions. In
[
CFU22
]
we already estimated

\Phi_{\mathfrak{z}_{1}}[u^{\#}_{M}]+|z_{1}|^{2}z_{1}
in a
dual space analogous to
X^{*,s}([0,T))
and, although it did not improve the
result of
[
DLM19
]
, it streamlined the proof and allowed for extensions to other dimensions and more general differential operators.
In the current manuscript, we substantially extend the
spaces
X^{s}
and
Y^{s}
from
[
CFU22
]
by introducing a larger
range of integrability exponents, allowing for

\mathfrak{c}\notin\{2,\infty\}
in (
2
) and thus in

Lemma

2.4
and in
Lemma

2.5
. This is
essential for obtaining sharp regularity estimates on
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
.

Our second central result and the second technical challenge is
proving the regularity estimates on the multilinear correction terms

\mathfrak{z}_{k}
postulated in

Theorem

1.3
. We want to estimate

\mathfrak{z}_{k}
in
Y^{\mu(k,S)}
norms, which are natural for solution of
(
1
) with the initial condition

f\in H^{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
having compact Fourier support with unit radius.
Heuristically, the functions
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
cannot have more than

\mu(k,S)
degrees of regularity, since for initial data of the form

\widehat{f}(\xi)=\mathbbm{1}_{B_{1}(k)}(\xi)
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, the Wiener randomization
does not provide any regularity improvement (see

Section

1.1
). However, the proof that stochastic initial
conditions behave like ones with unit-radius Fourier support requires
efficient bookkeeping provided by ternary trees, as described in

Section

6
. We then inductively
use deterministic bounds together with a multi-parameter stochastic
chaos estimate
Lemma

6.3
to prove the regularity
estimates of
Theorem

1.7
.

Finally, the scattering results of

Theorem

1.6
follow
directly from the fact that a solution
u
is fixed point of
(
1
) and from a dual estimate to the linear flow

f\mapsto e^{it\Delta}f
. However, as detailed in
Corollary

1.5
, our
results also provide a new perspective on scattering. Indeed, our
methods show that the classical scattering holds, that is, the
nonlinear dynamics approaches the linear ones, in
H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, a very
coarse norm. However,

Theorem

1.6
and

Corollary

1.5
contain more precise information as they
provide an explicit expansion of the scattering data in terms of the
initial conditions, with a more regular remainder term.

Organization of the paper

In
Section

2
, we introduce directional space-time norms and review
the Littlewood-Paley decomposition and Bernstein’s inequality. We also
recall classical Strichartz estimates for the flow of the linear
Schrödinger evolution
f\mapsto e^{it\Delta}f
. We conclude the section by proving two
crucial estimates for the linear Schrödinger evolution

f\mapsto e^{it\Delta}f
in terms of directional spaces: the directional maximal
function estimate
Lemma

2.4
and the directional smoothing
estimate
Lemma

2.5
.

In
Section

3
we define the spaces

X^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
Y^{S}
, and
X^{*,\mathfrak{s}}
and prove estimates on the solution of
the linear non-homogeneous Schrödinger equation in these
spaces. Equivalently, such bounds provide estimates on the Duhamel
iteration map (
1
). In
Propositions
3.3
and
3.4
, we obtain dual
bounds for linear flow, and mapping properties of the Duhamel iteration map (
1
) into classical spaces

C^{0}\big([0,T),H^{S}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
. These estimates are respectively needed to prove scattering, and the uniqueness of solutions, relating them to more classical continuous-in-time
L^{2}
-in-space -based notions.

Section

4
is devoted to the proof of bilinear
and multilinear estimates. We establish boundedness of pointwise
products of space-time functions in terms of the norms
X^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
Y^{S}
,
and
X^{*,\mathfrak{s}}
.

In
Section

5
contains the proof of the deterministic
Theorem

1.6
using a fixed point argument.

In
Section

6
is dedicated to
probabilistic estimates and to the proof of

Theorem

1.3
. The section begins by
defining ternary trees, an efficient bookkeeping tool to track the
dependence of the multilinear correction terms
\mathfrak{z}_{k}
on the
initial data. In this section we also state the multilinear chaos
estimate that is the starting point of our probabilistic
analysis. While this result is known, especially in the case of Gaussian random variables, we provide a self-contained elementary proof in
Appendix

A
.

Finally, in
Section

7
, we combine the deterministic
results with the probabilistic estimates to prove

Theorem

1.1
and

Theorem

1.3
.

1.4.
Notation

•

For two expressions
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{LHS}}\mathopen{}
and
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{RHS}}\mathopen{}
we write

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{LHS}}\mathopen{}\lesssim\!\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{RHS}}\mathopen{}
if there exists a constant
C>0
depending only
on the parameters of the problem such that
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{LHS}}\mathopen{}\leq C\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{RHS}}\mathopen{}
. For
example,
C
can always depend on the dimension
d
. If we want to
emphasize, that
C
may depend on a parameter
\varepsilon
we write

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{LHS}}\mathopen{}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{RHS}}\mathopen{}
. Often
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{LHS}}\mathopen{}
and

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{RHS}}\mathopen{}
are norms: then the implicit constant cannot depend on any
functions appearing in the bound.

•

We write
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{LHS}}\mathopen{}\approx\!\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{RHS}}\mathopen{}
, if
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{LHS}}\mathopen{}\lesssim\!\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{RHS}}\mathopen{}
and
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{RHS}}\mathopen{}\lesssim\!\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{LHS}}\mathopen{}
.

•

If
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{RHS}}\mathopen{}
is non-negative and infinite, then
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{LHS}}\mathopen{}\lesssim\!\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{RHS}}\mathopen{}
is true by default.

•

The Fourier transform of the function
f
is denoted by

\mathcal{F}(f)(\xi)=\widehat{f}(\xi)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)e^{-2\pi i\xi x}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}x.

The dimension
d
is deduced from context. The Fourier inversion
formula holds:

f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widehat{f}(\xi)e^{2\pi i\xi x}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}x.

•

When dealing with a function of multiple variables

\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\ni(x,y)\mapsto f(x,y)
we emphasize the variable in
which we are taking the Fourier transform by using the notation

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathcal{F}}\mathopen{}_{y}(f)(x,\eta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}f(x,y)e^{-2\pi i\eta y}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}y.

For functions
f:I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
that depend on a “time” and “space”
variables the Fourier transform is taken only in the space variable:

\widehat{f}(t,\xi)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t,x)e^{-2\pi i\xi x}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}x.

•

The symbol
O(\varepsilon)
stands for any function

[0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

|O(\varepsilon)|\lesssim\varepsilon
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
with a constant uniform in

\varepsilon
. The specific function intended by
O(\varepsilon)
can change from line
to line.

•

The ball of radius
r
and center
x
is denoted by
B_{r}(x)
;
if
x=0
we simply write
B_{r}
. The dimension of the ball is to be
understood from context.

•

For
p\geq 1
,
p^{\prime}
stands for the dual of
p
, that is,
\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{p}=1
.

•

The notation
\langle x\rangle\coloneqq(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
is the Japanese bracket.

•

For
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote

\langle\Delta\rangle^{\sigma/2}
the operator with the Fourier multiplier

(1+|2\pi\xi|^{4})^{\nicefrac{{\sigma}}{{4}}}
, that is,

\mathcal{F}(\langle\Delta\rangle^{\sigma/2}f)(\xi)=\langle 2\pi|\xi|^{2}\rangle^{\sigma/2}\widehat{f}(\xi)
. Then,

H^{\sigma}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the Sobolev space endowed with the norm

\|u\|_{H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}=\|\langle\Delta\rangle^{\sigma/2}u(x)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

•

We denote by
\mathbbm{1}_{A}
the characteristic function of a set
A
,
that is,
\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)=1
if
x\in A
and
\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)=0
otherwise. In
addition, if, for example,
x>y
then we write
\mathbbm{1}_{x>y}
to
indicate the function that is equal to
1
when
x>y
and vanishes
otherwise. The variable of the function is to be deduced from context.

•

We denote by
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{spt}}\mathopen{}(f)\coloneqq\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{clo}}\mathopen{}\left(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|f(x)|\neq 0\}\right)
the support of the function
f
, where
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{clo}}\mathopen{}(A)
denotes the closure of the set
A
.

•

We denote by
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{diam}}\mathopen{}(A)\coloneqq\sup_{x,y\in A}|x-y|
the diameter of a set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

•

Given two sets
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
the sum and difference sets
A\pm B
are given by

A\pm B:=\{a+b:a\in A,b\in B\}\,.

2.
The linear evolution

The solution of the linear Schrödinger equation

\begin{cases}(i\partial_{t}+\Delta)u=0&\text{ on }[0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
u(0,x)=f(x)\end{cases}

with
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is given by the linear Schrödinger evolution
group
u(t,x)\coloneqq e^{it\Delta}f(x)
, which can be expressed using the Fourier
inversion formula as follows:

e^{it\Delta}f(x)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{2\pi i\xi x-4\pi^{2}it|\xi|^{2}}\widehat{f}(\xi)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}\xi\,.

In this section we introduce function spaces to bound solutions to
(
2
). It is straightforward to see that the
map
f\mapsto(t\mapsto e^{it\Delta}f)
is continuous from

H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to

C^{0}(\mathbb{R};H^{\sigma}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap C^{1}(\mathbb{R};H^{\sigma-2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(see e.g. the
proof of
Proposition

3.3
). However,
the space-time norms in this section are better suited for controlling
the dispersive nature of the linear evolution.

We begin with a review of Strichartz estimates for solutions to
(
2
). To capture the regularity properties
of our solutions we use a Littlewood-Paley decomposition (dyadic
frequency annuli decomposition). We review the definitions and recall
basic results like Bernstein’s inequality. Finally, we introduce
directional space-time norms, which are the main functional framework
for our paper and we prove the estimates for the linear evolution
(
2
) in terms of these norms.

A space-time norm is any norm that involves integrals in both the
temporal (
t
) and spatial (
x
) variables. We refer to the space-time norms

\|u\|_{L_{t}^{p}L_{x}^{q}(I)}\coloneqq\Big(\int_{I}\|u(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}t\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}

as Strichartz norms. We say that a pair of exponents

(p,q)
is Strichartz-admissible if

\frac{2}{p}+\frac{d}{q}=\frac{d}{2},\qquad q\in\Big[2,\frac{2d}{d-2}\Big),\;p\in\big(2,\infty\big],

and we call admissible those Strichartz norms that have such pairs of
integration exponents. For space-time norms, we omit
\mathbb{R}^{d}
from the
notation and emphasize only the time interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
Str77
,
GV92
,
KT98
]
)
.

If
(p,q)
be a Strichartz-admissible pair (in the sense of
(
2
)), then for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have
that

\|e^{it\Delta}f\|_{L_{t}^{p}L_{x}^{q}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,,

where the implicit constant does not depend on
I
or
f
.

Littlewood-Paley projections
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}
,
N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
isolate the
behavior of a function at frequencies of order
N
. They provide us
with control over the differentiability properties of the solution to
(
2
) at different scales. Note that the
Littlewood-Paley projections
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}
below are different from the unit
scale projection
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{Q}}\mathopen{}_{n}
, introduced in
(
1.1
).

To define
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}
we fix a real valued cutoff function

\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{1})
with
\varphi(\xi)=1
on
B_{1-2^{-100}}
and set

\varphi_{N}(\xi)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\varphi(\xi)&\text{if }N=1,\\
\varphi\Big(\frac{\xi}{N}\Big)-\varphi\Big(\frac{\xi}{N/2}\Big)&\text{if }N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}},\end{cases}

so that for each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
it holds that

\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}}\varphi_{N}(\xi)=1
. For
N>1
we have

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{spt}}\mathopen{}\varphi_{N}\subset\overline{B_{N}\setminus B_{(1-2^{-100})N/2}},\qquad\varphi_{N}(\xi)=1\text{ on }B_{(1-2^{-100})N}\setminus B_{N/2}.

The Littlewood-Paley (approximate) projections are then obtained by setting

\widehat{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f}(\xi)\coloneqq\varphi_{N}(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi)\qquad\text{or, equivalently,}\qquad\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f=f*\widehat{\varphi_{N}}\,.

Consequently,
f=\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f
with convergence in

H^{\sigma}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
f\in H^{\sigma}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, as can be seen on the
Fourier side using Lebesgue dominated convergence. In this manuscript
we also introduce modified Littlewood-Paley projections

\widetilde{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}}_{N}
,
N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
, defined as

\widetilde{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}}_{N}=\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}N^{\prime}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}\\
\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}<\nicefrac{{N^{\prime}}}{{N}}<8\end{subarray}}}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N^{\prime}},

so that, conveniently,

\widetilde{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}}_{N}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}=\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}\widetilde{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}}_{N}=\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}
. One can also express

\widetilde{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}}_{N}f
as a convolution operator:

\widetilde{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}}_{N}f=f*\widehat{\chi_{N}}\quad\text{with}\quad\chi_{N}(\xi)\coloneqq\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}N^{\prime}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}\\
\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}<\nicefrac{{N^{\prime}}}{{N}}<8\end{subarray}}}\varphi_{N^{\prime}}(|\xi|).

Note that
\|\widehat{\varphi_{N}}\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim 1
and

\|\widehat{\chi_{N}}\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim 1
with an implicit constant independent of

N
, so the Littlewood-Paley projections
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}
and

\widetilde{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}}_{N}
behave like averaging operators at the scale
N^{-1}
.

Remark 2.2
.

The evolution group
e^{it\Delta}
commutes with the projections

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}
,
N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
, and
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{Q}}\mathopen{}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
(and, more
generally, with Fourier multiplier operators). Thus,
(
2.1
) also holds with
f
replaced by
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f
or
by
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{Q}}\mathopen{}_{n}f
on both sides of the inequality.

Bernstein’s inequality allows one to control the Lebesgue norms with
higher exponents by ones with lower exponents as long as the support
in frequency of the estimated function has bounded diameter.

Lemma 2.3

(Bernstein’s inequality)
.

For any
r_{1},r_{2}\in[1,\infty]
with
r_{2}\geq r_{1}
it holds that

\|f\|_{L^{r_{2}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{diam}}\mathopen{}\big(\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{spt}}\mathopen{}(\widehat{f})\big)^{\frac{d}{r_{1}}-\frac{d}{r_{2}}}\|f\|_{L^{r_{1}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

In particular, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
we obtain

\big\|\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{Q}}\mathopen{}_{k}f\big\|_{L^{r_{2}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\big\|\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{Q}}\mathopen{}_{k}f\big\|_{L^{r_{1}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

since
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{diam}}\mathopen{}\big(\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{spt}}\mathopen{}(\widehat{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{Q}}\mathopen{}_{k}f})\big)\lesssim 1
, and

\big\|\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f\big\|_{L^{r_{2}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq N^{d(\frac{1}{r_{1}}-\frac{1}{r_{2}})}\big\|\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f\big\|_{L^{r_{1}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

since
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{diam}}\mathopen{}\big(\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{spt}}\mathopen{}(\widehat{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f})\big)\lesssim N
. The implicit constants are allowed to depend only on the dimension,
r_{1}
, and
r_{2}
.

Finally, we introduce, directional space-time norms. These norms are designed to capture directional behavior of solutions to
(
2
). For any
l\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
we decompose

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as

x=x_{l}e_{l}+\sum_{i=1,i\neq l}^{d}x_{i}e_{i}=:x_{l}e_{l}+x^{\prime}_{l}.

Given a time interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and a fixed coordinate direction

l\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, the directional space-time norms

(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c})\in[1,\infty)^{3}
are given by

\|h\|_{L_{e_{l}}^{\left(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c}\right)}(I)}=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big(\int_{I}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}|h(t,x_{l}e_{l}+x_{l}^{\prime})|^{\mathfrak{c}}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}{x_{l}^{\prime}}\Big)^{\frac{\mathfrak{b}}{\mathfrak{c}}}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}t\Big)^{\frac{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{b}}}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}x_{l}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{a}}}\,,

where
x_{l}^{{}^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
is identified with a vector of

\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
0
for its
l
-th component. If

\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c}\}\ni\infty
we use the standard modifications by the
essential supremum norm. We refer to directional norms

L_{e_{l}}^{(\mathfrak{a},\infty,\mathfrak{c})}(I)
as “directional maximal norms”
because of the supremum in
t\in I
. When
\mathfrak{b}=2
we refer to the
norms as “directional smoothing norms” due to the negative power of

N
appearing below in (
2.5
), which manifests
dampening of high oscillations, or smoothing.

The following lemmata are generalizations of
[
CFU22
, Lemma 2.4 and Lemma
2.5]
and they establish bounds on solutions to
(
2
) in terms of directional norms from
(
2
).
Lemma

2.4
is
a directional estimate maximal estimate, whereas

Lemma

2.5
is a smoothing
result.

Lemma 2.4
.

Fix
N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
,
l\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, and
\mathfrak{c}\in(\mathfrak{c}_{0},\infty]
with
\mathfrak{c}_{0}\coloneqq 2\frac{d-1}{d-2}
. For any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have that

\big\|e^{it\Delta}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f\big\|_{L_{e_{l}}^{(2,\infty,\mathfrak{c})}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim_{\mathfrak{c}}N^{\frac{1}{2}+\big(\frac{d-2}{2}-\frac{d-1}{\mathfrak{c}}\big)}\|\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

Furthermore, if
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{diam}}\mathopen{}\big(\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{spt}}\mathopen{}(\widehat{f})\big)\leq 2R
, then for any
\varepsilon>0
we obtain that

\big\|e^{it\Delta}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f\big\|_{L_{e_{l}}^{(2,\infty,\mathfrak{c})}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim_{R,\varepsilon}N^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}\|\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The implicit constants are allowed to depend on
\mathfrak{c}
,
R>0
, and

\varepsilon>0
, but are independent of
N
and the function
f
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Fix

N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
,
l\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, and
\mathfrak{c}\in[2,\infty]
. Define the directional frequency
cone projections as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{U}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{e_{l}}f}(\xi)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{U}_{e_{l}}}(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi),\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{U}_{e_{l}}\coloneqq\Big\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\frac{|\xi_{l}|}{|\xi|}>\frac{1}{2\sqrt{d}}\Big\}\setminus\bigcup_{l^{\prime}=1}^{l-1}\Big\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\colon\frac{|\xi_{l^{\prime}}|}{|\xi|}>\frac{1}{2\sqrt{d}}\Big\}\,.

Then, for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have that

\|e^{it\Delta}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{U}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{e_{l}}f\|_{L_{e_{l}}^{(\infty,2,\mathfrak{c})}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\big(\frac{d-1}{2}-\frac{d-1}{\mathfrak{c}}\big)}\|\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{U}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{e_{l}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

and if
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{diam}}\mathopen{}\big(\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{spt}}\mathopen{}(\widehat{f})\big)\leq 2R
, then

\big\|e^{it\Delta}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{U}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{e_{l}}f\big\|_{L_{e_{l}}^{(\infty,2,\mathfrak{c})}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim_{R}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{U}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{e_{l}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

The implicit constants are allowed to depend on
\mathfrak{c}
and
R>0
but are independent of
N
and the function
f
.

Clearly, by replacing the time interval
\mathbb{R}
by any
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, the
space-time norms on the left-hand sides of the estimates in

Lemma

2.1
,
Lemma

2.4
, and

Lemma

2.5
decrease, so local-in-time versions of the
bounds above also trivially hold.

Proof of
Lemma

2.4
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
l=1
so that
x=(x_{1},x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
. For any fixed
(t,x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
,
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, and any
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the Fourier transform in the
x^{\prime}
variable as

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathcal{F}}\mathopen{}_{x^{\prime}}g(t,x_{1},\xi^{\prime})\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}g(t,x_{1},x^{\prime})e^{-2\pi i\xi^{\prime}x^{\prime}}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}x^{\prime}\,.

First, we claim that (
2.4
) follows from (
2.4
) via a fiberwise application of Bernstein’s inequality (
Lemma

2.3
). Indeed, if
\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{diam}}\mathopen{}\big(\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{spt}}\mathopen{}(\hat{f})\big)\leq 2R
, then there exists
\widetilde{\xi}=(\widetilde{\xi}_{1},\widetilde{\xi}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\hat{f}(\xi)=0
whenever
|\widetilde{\xi}-\xi|>2R
and in particular,
\hat{f}(\xi_{1},\xi^{\prime})=0
whenever
|\widetilde{\xi}^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime}|>2R
. Since the evolution group
e^{it\Delta}
conserves the Fourier support, then

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathcal{F}}\mathopen{}_{x^{\prime}}(e^{it\Delta}f)(x_{1},\xi^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi i\xi_{1}x_{1}-4\pi^{2}it(\xi_{1}^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|^{2})}\hat{f}(\xi_{1},\xi^{\prime})\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}\xi_{1}=0

whenever
|\widetilde{\xi}^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime}|>2R
, and therefore

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{diam}}\mathopen{}\Big(\big\{\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\colon\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathcal{F}}\mathopen{}_{x^{\prime}}(e^{it\Delta}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f)(x_{1},\xi^{\prime})\neq 0\big\}\Big)\leq 4R.

Then, by Bernstein’s inequality (
Lemma

2.3
), for given
\varepsilon>0
and
\mathfrak{c}
it holds that

\|e^{it\Delta}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f(x_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}\lesssim_{R}\|e^{it\Delta}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f(x_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{c}}}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}\,,

where
\widetilde{\mathfrak{c}}\in(2,\mathfrak{c})
is chosen to satisfy
\frac{d-2}{2}-\frac{d-1}{\widetilde{\mathfrak{c}}}<\varepsilon
.
The claimed bound (
2.4
) then follows from (
2.4
).

Next, we focus on the proof of (
2.4
), which is based on a
TT^{*}
argument and classical techniques for oscillatory integrals.
Let us introduce the operator
T_{N}
given by

(T_{N}f)(t,\cdot)\coloneqq e^{it\Delta}\widetilde{P}_{N}f\,,

where
\widetilde{P}_{N}
is as in (
2
).
Then,
T_{N}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f=e^{it\Delta}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{P}}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathopen{}_{N}f
and (
2.4
) follows once we prove the estimate

\|T_{N}f\|_{L_{e_{1}}^{(2,\infty,\mathfrak{c})}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim N^{\frac{1}{2}+(\frac{d-2}{2}-\frac{d-1}{\mathfrak{c}})}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

and replace
f
by
P_{N}f
. By duality, (
2
) is equivalent to

\|T^{*}_{N}g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim N^{\frac{1}{2}+(\frac{d-2}{2}-\frac{d-1}{\mathfrak{c}})}\|g\|_{L_{e_{l}}^{(2,1,\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})}(\mathbb{R})}\,,

where
T^{*}_{N}
is the dual operator to
T_{N}
with respect to the

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
inner product. We claim that (
2
) follows by showing the
TT^{*}
bound

\|T_{N}T_{N}^{*}g\|_{L_{e_{l}}^{(2,\infty,\mathfrak{c})}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim N^{1+2(\frac{d-2}{2}-\frac{d-1}{\mathfrak{c}})}\|g\|_{L_{e_{l}}^{(2,1,\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})}(\mathbb{R})}.

Indeed, if (
2
) holds, then

\|T_{N}^{*}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\begin{aligned} &=|\langle T_{N}^{*}g,T_{N}^{*}g\rangle|=|\langle T_{N}T_{N}^{*}g,g\rangle|\\
&\leq\|T_{N}T_{N}^{*}g\|_{L_{e_{l}}^{(2,\infty,\mathfrak{c})}(\mathbb{R}\times)}\|g\|_{L_{e_{l}}^{(2,1,\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})}(\mathbb{R})}\\
&\lesssim N^{1+2(\frac{d-2}{2}-\frac{d-1}{\mathfrak{c}})}\|g\|^{2}_{L_{e_{l}}^{\left(2,1,\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}\right)}(\mathbb{R})}\end{aligned}

and (
2
) follows.

To prove (
2
) we may assume that
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, as the general result follows by standard approximation arguments. Using the Fourier inversion representation of the Schrödinger evolution group (
2
) and the defining identity

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(T_{N}^{*}g)(x)\overline{f(x)}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(x)\overline{(T_{N}f)(t,x)}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}t\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}x\,,

we can express
T_{N}
and
T_{N}^{*}
as

\displaystyle(T_{N}f)(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{2\pi i\xi x-4\pi^{2}it|\xi|^{2}}\chi_{N}(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi)d\xi.

\displaystyle(T_{N}^{*}g)(x)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{2\pi i\xi x+4\pi^{2}it|\xi|^{2}}\widehat{g}(t,\xi)\chi_{N}(\xi)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}t\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}\xi.

Combining the two expressions in (
2
) followed by a further use of the Fourier inversion formula shows that

\displaystyle T_{N}T_{N}^{*}g(t,x)=

\displaystyle K_{N}*g(t,x)=\;\int_{\mathrlap{\hskip-8.19447pt\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}}K_{N}(t-s,x-y)g(s,y)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}s\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}y,

\displaystyle K_{N}(t,x)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{2\pi i\xi x-4\pi^{2}it|\xi|^{2}}\chi_{N}^{2}(\xi)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}\xi.

For fixed
(t,x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, let
\|K_{N}(t,x_{1},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{p^{\prime}},L^{p})}
denote the operator norm of the map

h\mapsto\int_{\mathclap{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}}K_{N}(t,x_{1},x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})h(y^{\prime})\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}y^{\prime}

from
L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
to
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
. To prove (
2
), it suffices to prove the bounds

(2.24)

\displaystyle\|K_{N}(t,x_{1},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{1},L^{\infty})}\lesssim N^{d}(1+N|x_{1}|)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\,,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\|K_{N}(t,x_{1},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2},L^{2})}\lesssim N(1+N|x_{1}|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,.

with implicit constants independent of any fixed
(t,x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
.
Indeed, the Riesz-Thorin interpolation theorem applied to the bounds
(
2.24
) and (
2.25
) yields, for
any fixed
(t,x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\mathfrak{c}\geq 2
, that

\|K_{N}(t,x_{1},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}},L^{\mathfrak{c}})}\lesssim N^{\frac{2+\mathfrak{c}d-2d}{\mathfrak{c}}}(1+N|x_{1}|)^{-\frac{1}{2}-(d-1)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{c}})}.

Therefore, if
\mathfrak{c}>\mathfrak{c}_{0}\coloneqq 2\frac{d-1}{d-2}
, then

-\frac{1}{2}-(d-1)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{c}})<-1
, and consequently

\Big\|\big\|\|K_{N}(t,x_{1},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{\mathfrak{c^{\prime}}},L^{\mathfrak{c})}}\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(\mathbb{R})}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{x_{1}}(\mathbb{R})}\leq N^{1+2\big(\frac{d-2}{2}-\frac{d-1}{\mathfrak{c}}\big)}.

Hence, Young’s convolution inequality in
t
implies for any

x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
that

\displaystyle\big\|\|K_{N}*g(t,x_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|\int_{\mathclap{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}}\|K_{N}(t-s,x_{1}-y_{1},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}},L^{\mathfrak{c}})}\|g(s,y_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}s\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}y_{1}\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big\|\|K_{N}(t,x_{1}-y_{1},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}},L^{\mathfrak{c}})}\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(\mathbb{R})}\;\big\|\|g(t,y_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}\big\|_{L^{1}_{t}(\mathbb{R})}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}y_{1}.

Another application of Young’s convolution inequality, this time in
x_{1}
, gives us

\displaystyle\Big\|\big\|\|K_{N}*g(t,x_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(\mathbb{R})}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{x_{1}}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|\big\|\|K_{N}(t,x_{1},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}},L^{\mathfrak{c}})}\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(\mathbb{R})}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{x_{1}}(\mathbb{R})}\|g\|_{L_{e_{1}}^{(2,1,\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})}(\mathbb{R})},

and (
2
) follows from (
2
).

To show (
2.24
), we use the rescaling

\xi\to N\xi
and
\chi_{1}(\xi)=\chi_{N}(N\xi)
in (
2
) to obtain

K_{N}(t,x)=N^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{2\pi iN\xi x-4\pi^{2}iN^{2}t|\xi|^{2}}\chi_{1}^{2}(\xi)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}\xi.

The function
\chi_{1}
is a bump function independent of
N
for any

N\geq 4
. By interchanging the absolute value and integral, we obtain
a trivial bound
\big|K_{N}(t,x)\big|\lesssim N^{d}
, while using Stein’s Lemma

[
SM93
, Section 5.13, p363]
we obtain for any

(t,x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
that

\big|K_{N}(t,x)\big|\lesssim N^{d}\big(N|x|+N^{2}|t|\big)^{-\frac{d}{2}}.

A combination with the trivial bound yields

\big|K_{N}(t,x_{1},x^{\prime})\big|\begin{aligned} &\lesssim N^{d}(1+N|x_{1}|+N|x^{\prime}|+N^{2}|t|)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\\
&\lesssim N^{d}(1+N|x_{1}|)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\end{aligned}

and (
2.24
) follows from Young’s convolution
inequality in
x^{\prime}
.

To show (
2.25
), by Plancherel’s identity in the
x^{\prime}

variable, we have for any fixed
(t,x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
that

\|K_{N}(t,x_{1},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2},L^{2})}\begin{aligned} &=\sup_{\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}\leq 1}\|K_{N}(t,x_{1},\cdot)*f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}\\
&=\sup_{\|\hat{f}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}\leq 1}\|\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathcal{F}}\mathopen{}_{x^{\prime}}K_{N}(t,x_{1},\cdot)\widehat{f}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}\\
&\leq\|\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathcal{F}}\mathopen{}_{x^{\prime}}K_{N}(t,x_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}\,.\end{aligned}

Using Fourier inversion and the change of variables

\xi_{1}\to N\xi_{1}
we obtain for any fixed
(t,x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\eta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
that

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathcal{F}}\mathopen{}_{x^{\prime}}{K}_{N}(t,x_{1},\eta^{\prime})\begin{aligned} &=\int_{\mathclap{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}}K_{N}(t,x_{1},x^{\prime})e^{-2\pi i\eta^{\prime}x^{\prime}}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}x^{\prime}\\
&=\int_{\mathclap{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}}\int_{\;\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{2\pi i\xi(x_{1},x^{\prime})-4\pi^{2}it|\xi|^{2}}\chi^{2}_{N}(\xi)e^{-2\pi i\eta^{\prime}x^{\prime}}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}\xi\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}x^{\prime}\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi i\xi_{1}x_{1}-4\pi^{2}it\xi_{1}^{2}-4\pi^{2}it|\eta^{\prime}|^{2}}\chi^{2}_{N}(\xi_{1},\eta^{\prime})\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}\xi_{1}\\
&=N\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi iN\xi_{1}x_{1}-4\pi^{2}itN^{2}\xi_{1}^{2}-4\pi^{2}it|\eta^{\prime}|^{2}}\chi_{1}^{2}\big(\xi_{1},\eta^{\prime}/N\big)\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathrm{d}}\mathopen{}\xi_{1}.\end{aligned}

Since
\xi_{1}\mapsto\chi_{1}^{2}\big(\xi_{1},\eta^{\prime}/N\big)
is
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
function
bounded by 1,
Stein’s lemma
[
SM93
, Section
5.13, p363]
