follow from the construction of
\pi_{r}
. To show
(iii)
, fix
r\in\{1,\ldots,2p\}
and suppose that

\pi_{r}(\hat{e}_{\nu})=\pi_{r}(\hat{e}_{\nu^{\prime}})=\hat{e}_{j}\neq 0
for some
j
and
\nu,\nu^{\prime}
. Then, by (
A
),
m_{\nu}=(\vec{k}_{r})_{j}=m_{\nu^{\prime}}
. Since
\vec{m}\in\Delta^{N}
, then
m_{\nu}=m_{\nu^{\prime}}
implies that
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
and (iii) follows. Finally, fix any
\hat{e}_{\nu}\in\mathcal{E}^{N}
. Since, by definition,
m_{\nu}
appears among the coefficients of
(\vec{k}_{r})
and
(\vec{k}_{1},\cdots,\vec{k}_{2p})\not\in\mathscr{Z}^{2p,n}
, then there are at least two pairs
(r,j)
and
(r^{\prime},j^{\prime})
such that
m_{\nu}=(\vec{k}_{r})_{j}=(\vec{k}_{r^{\prime}})_{j^{\prime}}
. Then,
\pi_{r}(\hat{e}_{\nu})=e_{j}\neq 0
and
\pi_{r^{\prime}}(\hat{e}_{\nu})=e_{j^{\prime}}\neq 0
and (iv) is proved.

Overall, we proved that
\vec{\pi}\in\mathscr{P}_{N\to n,2}^{2p}
, and therefore (
A
) is established.

Then (
A.2
) and (
A
) yield

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\big[|H|^{2p}\big]\leq C_{2p,n,\vec{\alpha}}\sum_{N=n}^{2pn}\sum_{\vec{\pi}\in\mathscr{P}^{2p}_{N\to n,2}}\sum_{\vec{m}\in\Delta^{N}}\Big(\prod_{r=1}^{2p}|c(\pi_{r}(\vec{m}))|\Big)\,.

Finally, using
Theorem

A.4
on the inner sum we obtain that

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\big[|H|^{2p}\big]\leq C_{2p,n,\vec{\alpha}}\sum_{N=n}^{2pn}\sum_{\vec{\pi}\in\mathscr{P}^{2p}_{N\to n,2}}\|c\|_{l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})}^{2p}.

Since, for fixed
p,N,n
the number of maps in
\mathscr{P}^{2p}_{N\to n,2}
is finite, we have

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\big[|H|^{2p}\big]\leq C_{2p,n,\vec{\alpha}}|\mathscr{P}^{2p}_{N\to n,2}|\|c\|_{l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})}^{2p}\,,

as desired.
∎

Let us deduce
Theorem

A.1
from

Lemma

A.5
.

Proof of
Theorem

A.1
.

Let
L_{\alpha}\coloneqq\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{d}
and

L_{\beta}\coloneqq\min\{L_{\alpha},L_{g}\}
, where we recall that
L_{g}
is the dimension of the space of polynomials in
L^{2}(\mathbb{C},\mu_{g})
. First, we provide the proof assuming that there exists a constant
f_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
and finitely supported functions
f_{n^{\prime},\vec{\beta}}:\Delta^{n^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{C}
with
n^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
\vec{\beta}\in(\mathbb{N}_{L_{\beta}}\setminus\{0\})^{n^{\prime}}
, such that

H=f_{0}+\sum_{n^{\prime}=1}^{n}\sum_{\vec{\beta}\in(\mathbb{N}_{L_{\beta}}\setminus\{0\})^{n^{\prime}}}\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Delta^{n^{\prime}}}f_{n^{\prime},\vec{\beta}}(\vec{k})P^{\vec{\beta}}_{n^{\prime}}(g_{\vec{k}}).

Indeed, if (
A
) holds, then (
A.2
)
implies

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\big[H^{2}\big]=|f_{0}|^{2}+\sum_{n^{\prime}=1}^{n}\sum_{\vec{\beta}\in(\mathbb{N}_{L_{\beta}}\setminus\{0\})^{n^{\prime}}}\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Delta^{n^{\prime}}}|f_{n^{\prime},\vec{\beta}}(\vec{k})|^{2}\,,

where we used (
A.2
) used that the random variables
P^{\vec{\beta}}_{k}(g_{\vec{n}})
. Next, for any
p\in[2,\infty)
the Minkowski inequality yields

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\big[H^{p}\big]^{1/p}\leq|f_{0}|+\sum_{n^{\prime}=1}^{n}\sum_{\vec{\beta}\in(\mathbb{N}_{L_{\beta}}\setminus\{0\})^{n^{\prime}}}\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\Big[\Big|\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Delta^{n^{\prime}}}f_{n^{\prime},\vec{\beta}}(\vec{k})P^{\vec{\beta}}_{n^{\prime}}(\vec{k})\Big|^{p}\Big]^{1/p}

and from
Lemma

A.5
it follows that

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\big[H^{p}\big]^{1/p}\lesssim|f_{0}|+\sum_{n^{\prime}=1}^{n}\sum_{\vec{\beta}\in(\mathbb{N}_{L_{\beta}}\setminus\{0\})^{n^{\prime}}}\Big(\sum_{\vec{k}\in\Delta^{n^{\prime}}}|f_{n^{\prime},\vec{\beta}}(\vec{k})|^{2}\Big)^{1/2},

with the implicit constant dependents on
n
,
p
, and
L_{\beta}
, and
the distribution of
g
. Since the sums over
k
and
\vec{\beta}
are finite, it follows from the Cauchy inequality that

\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\big[H^{p}\big]^{1/p}\lesssim\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\big[H^{2}\big]^{1/2}
, as desired.

To prove that any
H
as in the statement of
Theorem

A.1
can be represented as (
A
) we first focus on
the special case
H=\prod_{j=1}^{n}g_{k_{j}}^{\alpha_{j}}
for some
(k_{j})_{j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

By grouping together multiple occurrences of the same random variables
g_{k}
, for some
k
, we obtain

H=\prod_{j=1}^{\widetilde{n}}g_{m_{j}}^{\gamma_{j}}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}g_{k_{j}}^{\alpha_{j}},

for some
\widetilde{n}\leq n
, some distinct
(m_{j})_{j\in\{1,\ldots,\widetilde{n}\}}
with

\{m_{1},\ldots m_{\widetilde{n}}\}=\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\}
, and
\gamma_{1}+\ldots+\gamma_{\widetilde{n}}=\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}
. In particular,
\gamma_{j}\leq L_{\alpha}
. Since
m_{1},\ldots,m_{\widetilde{n}}
are distinct, by permuting the random variables, we can without loss of generality assume
m_{1}<m_{2}<\ldots<m_{\widetilde{n}}
, and therefore
\vec{m}\in\Delta^{\widetilde{n}}
.
In addition, since the polynomials
(P^{\beta})_{\beta\in\{0,\ldots,L_{\beta}\}}
, span the
space of polynomials of degree at most
L_{\alpha}
(as
L^{2}(\mathbb{C};\mu_{g})

functions), then for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,\widetilde{n}\}
there exist complex
coefficients
(\lambda_{j,\beta}\in\mathbb{C})_{\beta=0}^{L_{\beta}}
such that

g^{\gamma_{j}}_{m}=\lambda_{j,0}+\sum_{\beta=1}^{L_{\beta}}\lambda_{j,\beta}P^{\beta}(g_{m}).

for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Thus,

H=\prod_{j=1}^{\widetilde{n}}g_{m_{j}}^{\gamma_{j}}\begin{aligned} &=\prod_{j=1}^{\widetilde{n}}\Big(\lambda_{j,0}+\sum_{\beta_{j}=1}^{L_{\beta}}\lambda_{j,\beta_{j}}P^{\beta_{j}}(g_{m_{j}})\Big)\end{aligned}

and (
A
) follows for our special
H
after expanding the product. The general case follows from linearity and the proof is completed.
∎
